« New York, July 1, 

“Mr J. Pierpont Morgan, who is 
now: 68 years of age, the American 
iron and steel magnate, has been 
taken ill. 

- To-day he was Seiaved, from his 
sister’ home at West Manchester, 
Massachussetts, to his Long Island 
Estate. by ambulance and” private 
rail coach. 

His sister,declares that the lll- 
ness is slight.—

“Daddy won't be home to dinner 
this evening, Willie, ‘so, you will 
have to take his place.” 

“Do you mean Tl have to read: 
the paper ‘at the table?” . 


Father: “First: of all, my boy, 
realize that my time is limited. 
Secondly, 
Thirdly, be, short.” 

Son, “Well, dad, first, I do. 
Secondly. I will, Thirdly, I am.” 

. . . . 

Two Irishmen came to a.ratlroad 
crossing. The gates were down. 

Stopping the’ car, they settled 
down until the train should pass, 
but both were soon asleep. 

With thundering wheels the ex- 
press dashed past, causing them to 
open their eyes. 

“T say,” said one, “wasn't that a 
well-lighted village we passed. 
through?” 

“Yes,” _ yawned his cortiganton: 
“an’ did: you notice that the first’ 
house was on fire?” 

. : o ee" o 

My nephew came running into 
the house, exclatming, “I lost my 
penny over by the big house!” 

Pointing! I said, “You mean that 
big brick house?” ; 

“No, this ‘house has bricks dnly 
up to its knees.”

“Through the building of the 
Baltic Port of Gdynia, Poland has 
become a maritime power, and 
nothing will ever drive her from 
the position she has thus attain: 
ed,” declared the President of the 
Polish ' Republic, _Moscicki, ad- 
dressing a gathering on the oc- 
casion of the annual “Day of the 
ea” at Gdynis... The (President 
added that Poland jis willing to 
co-operate with ‘all nations, parti- 
cularly ‘with ‘her Baltic neigh- 
bours, but is aware.of-tne fact 
that not. treaties but only the na- 
ilon’s own strength afford suf- 
Seient protection. --- ‘

_ A VEXED QUESTION 

A question often raised is why 
fast ‘Majl Only’services are not 
provided, leaving passenger and 
freight trafic to be carried by 
slower: aircraft. The possiblities 
of this system of operation ‘have, 
o{ course, been exhaustively ex- 
amined, and !t has ‘been: found to 
be unsuitable for the peculiar: 
conditions of Empire air transport. 
unquestionable rea- 


ter than the amount coming into 
the United Kingdon from them, 
so that a ‘Mail Only’'service vould 
be left with .unsaleable capacity. 
Further, ' mai! loads vary from 
week to week, while the ameunt of 
postal traffic at Christmas is ex- 
ceptionally high. so that if mail 
alone is carried no latitude ts left 
for the adjustment of loads ‘trom 
week to week or in the two direc- 
tions, and this system would make 
permanent a low load factor and 
thereforé 2 much higher cost per 
unit of welght carried;, this must 
be avolded if financial indepen- 
dence is to be hopéd for at an early 
date. | 

Secondly, the composite load’ 
system permits the employment 
ot large aircraft which by’ com- 
parison’ with, small aircraft show 


an improvement in the cost of’ 


carrying a unit of weight through 
a unit of distance. The larger 
aircraft necessary under this sys- 
‘tem permit of the provision of 
‘more comfortable passenger amen- 
{tles and are thus more attractlye 
to passengers, it- being a well 
established fact that passenger 
trafic grayitates naturally . to- 
wards the ‘Companies employing 


‘the larger types of aircraft. 


Thirdly, the separation of mails | 


fram passengers jwould not per- 
imit of as great a. frequency «of 
services as under the ° ‘composite 
load’ system, and, ds ‘already 
mentioned, increased’ frequency 1s 
as important—if not ‘more ims’ 
Portant—than reductions in over- 
all transit times.“

They are high wing flying boat 
monoplanes with a fully’ laden 
weight of nearly 18 tons.and have 
a pay load (that is, a saleable 
capacity), of . between 3)-4 tons 
under normal operating condi- 
tions. Eath flying boat will have 
4 Pegasus engines each of 740 Lp. 
giving ‘the aircraft: a top speed 
in the neighbourhood of 200 miles 
an hour. In the forepart of the’ 
hull there are two decks; at the 
forward end of the upper deck 1s 
the control room, immediately. 
behind Js the radio station, and 
abaft that ls the mall hold in 
which can be stowed three tons of 
‘mall, there being arrangements 
for dealing with the mail during 
filght.» On the starboard side of 
the mail hold is the navigating 
compartment and the ship's of- 
fice. In the forepeak is the moor-' 
ing compartment, and att of this 
ls the smoking cabin cn the lower 

, deck, Incidentally,: it is interest- 
Ing tc note that these aircraft for 
the first time provide smoking 
facilities cn the ‘Empire routes. 
Behind. the smoking cabin comes 
the kitchen; aft of this ty the mid- 
ship cabin, then the promenade 
saloon’ and finally the after cabin. 
Freight ang baggage Is stowed 
rght aft in a special hold. 


DAY TIME FLYING 


At present flying is done during 
the day, time und passengers sleep 
in hotels at night, but as already. 
mentioned, under'the new scheme 
day afd night’ fying will be car- 
tied on so that the Empire flying 
boats are. Atted with . Sleeping: 
berths as well as with chairs. 
There will be sleeping accommoca 
tlon for sixteen passengers but It. 
will’ be possible to take an addl- 
tional eight passengers wver the 
,day stages only. It is also inter- 
"esting to note that these 28 flying 
ships Involve a capital outlay run- 
ning well into seven figures, this 
order being undoubtedly the lar- 
gest ever placed at any one time 
for commercial aircraft,’ 

Each flying boat will be equip- 
ped with the most modern. type 
of navigational instruments in- 
cluding the automatic pilot, blind- 
flying equipment and radio instal- 
lation, making it possible to oper-. 
ate these trunk services 9 sche- 
dule even during adverse weather 
conditions.

toute with a unique type of sir- 
eraft the construction of which «is

(Speciai Air Mail Service) 
» London, June 23. 
The,King, who assumed the title 
of Captain-General and Colonel of 
the “Honourable .Artillery .Com- 
pany shortly after his accession, 


‘lias signified his intention of re- 


verting to old custom and being 
known simply as Captain-General. 
The Company's first Cabtain- 
General was James, Duke of York. 
afterwards James ‘IL, and .the 
rank was subsequently borne by 
Kings and other members of the 
Royal Family, until William IV. 
* The Duke of Sussex, who suc- 
ceeded King William in the rank, 
was already Colonel of the HAC, 
and consequeritly became known 


.as-.Captain-General .and Colonel, 


as were his successors until King 
George V 

The only other, Captaih;General 
in the British Empire is the 
Governor of Jamaica, where this 
“old military title has been in use 
since Cromwellian days. The rank 
is coraparatively common In Spain, 
where ,it originated. :

in the lack of engine power. But

| Death Of Well-Known 
Border Man: * 


London, June 10. 


The death occurred at his resid~ |. 


ence," Makerstoun House, near 
Kelso, on Saturday, after an ill- 
ness of about three months, of Mr 
James J. Bell-Irving, in his’ 77th 
year. A son of the late Mr John 
Bell-Irving, of Whitehill, Dumfries~ 
shire, Mr Bell-Irving spent the ear- 
Uer part of his life in China, being 
head of the firm of Jardine, Ma- 
thieson and Co., He was ‘a mem- 
ber of the Legislative .and Execu- 
‘tive Councils of Hong Kong, and 
was chairman and director of 
numerous: public companies. 


‘. When. he retumed from China 
he took up residexice at Barnara 
Castle, Yorkshite, where he resid- 
ed until he acquired the Makers- 
toun Estate:in 1820. Since then he 
took Mttle part in public lfe, but 
was very well-known in sporting 
circles. We was the owner of race 
horses, and was keenly interested 
in voursing. A good shot and an 


“expert angler, he was also intérest- 


ed in farming, particularly in Bel- 
ted Galloway cattle. ° 


Mr Bell-Irving, ‘who was a stew-. 


ard at the Carlisle, Edinburgh, ‘and 
Lanark race fixtures, won his last 
race last week, when his filly, Ivy 
Grey, was successful in the Whit- 
suntide Foal Stakes at Manchester 
on Wednesday. He won the- An- 
niversary Handicap at’ Derby last 
year with Pink Wings."  — 

Mr Bell-Irving is survived. by his 
wife and two daughters. His wife, 
whom he married in Hong. Kong 
in 1890, was Miss .Eva Gertrude 
Plercy of Marchwiel Hall, Wrex- 
ham, She took” a very active in- 
terest in public affairs, and isa 
member: of Roxburgh ‘County 
Council. The younger’ daughter is 
Mrs Ormrod, who resides with her 
busband ‘in the South of England, 
while the elder daughter js Coun- 
tess of Lauderdale. | 2 ard

BUDGET TRIBUNAL 
7 BEVEREN CE 


— 
act 


“ ude London! June 18." 
A suggestion that the procedure, 
in regard to, criminal trials in Eng-. 
land should be ‘brought into lne 
with that in Scotland was rejected 
by the Attorney-Gerieral (Sir Don- 
ald Somervell) in the House of 
Commons yesterday. 
Mr Cassels, the Labour member 
for Dumbartonshire, asked the At- 
torney-General whether, having 
regard to the impossibility. of -ob- 
taining a jury unfamiliar with the 
earlier findings of the Budget Tri- 
bunal, he was..prepared to recom- 
mend ‘legislation applicable to cri- 
minal trials in England, bringing 
the same into line with that at 
present in existence in Scotland. 


Sir Donald Somervell ! “replied 


referring to proceedings in a cri- 
minal trial which took place be- 
fore committing justices. The pro- 
cedure of, the Budget Tribunal was 
quite different from the procedure 
In & case before comuhitting” jus- 
tices, 


THE DIFFERENCE 

pe a 
The Budget Tribunal .made ex- 
press finditigs of fact and the evid- 


admissible against persons charged 
with specific offences before com- 
mitting justices. Committing jus. 
tices made. no findings of fact, but 
determined ° whether there was a 
casé to be sent for trial elsewhere. 
With regard to the last part of 


land was wholly different from 
that of England. Without suggest- 
ing any criticism of Scottish pro- 
cedure, he was not prepared to fre- 
commenc legislation to substitute 
“that Procedure for our own. 

Mr Mathers, another Scottish 
Labour member, asked if the At- 
torney-General suggested that in 
the light of the procedure in Eng- 
land in‘ regard to murder trials, he 
eduld criticise ‘the Scottish prac- 
tice. 


UNBIASED JURIES | 

Sir Donald Somervell—The last 
thing I should’ desire to dois to 
criticise the Scottish: practice, with 
which, indeed, I am nat perfectly 
familiar. rn 

~Mr Cassells asked if. the At- 
torney-General suggested that, 
with the legal procedure as it ex- 
; sted in England to-day, they could 
‘have an unblaged jury sitting on 
'.a murder trial, as had beer sug- 
j gested by Mr Mathers. . 

‘The Attorney-General—I cer- 
tainly would repudiate any sugges- 
tion ‘that juries who sit ‘on murder 
‘trials were not capable of bringing 
unbiased minds to bear on them, 
if the hon. members could make | 
any practicable suggestion to com-. 
bine the advantages of pubilcity 
with those of privacy he would be 
glad to conaider it, 0

he thinks they will be destroyed 
by aerial, bombardment in the 
next ‘war. Secondly, he thinks 
Britain might hand ovér the 
Queen Mary to America in part 
payment for her war debts. 

Mr. Griffin and his fami‘ly 4ar- 
rived in the Red Star liner Penn- 
land, which ‘called at Southamp- 
ton from New York yesterday.” , 
’ Mr, Griffin (the pessimist) said 
—"It is obvious that the impte- 
ments of modern warfare wil) not 
permit the present capitals ‘o 
remain forevar as they now stand, 
and the probability is ‘that future 
capitals will, be underground

ing to war debts, sald—"In the 
case of Britain it would“ be a 


gesture if, the Queen Mary, whlch}. 


was completed with the, ald of a 
Goverriment_ stibsidy, was . handed

of war debts. It cannot be caicu- 
lated to create good feelings, when

creased to time and one-fifth, 
with time and’ one-third for the 
firsc two hours of overtime and 
time anda half for every two 
hours worked thereafter. 

The unions agree to make no 
further applications for wage {n- 
creases in particular areas or 
sections of the industry for six 
months from June 29. The 
agreement must come before & 
delegate .conference of the. Amal- 
gamated Engineering Union, but 
its acceptance is regarded as cer- 
tain. ,

‘THE MEDITERRANEAN PROBLEM 


(BY HECTOR C. BYWATER) 


(Special Air Mall Service) 


London, June 23. , 


Important decisions relating to Empire defence are 
expected as the result of the visit to London of Mr. Os- 


- wald Pirow. the South African Minister for Transport : 


and Défence. Tans 


His’ statement that Cape Town 
will in no circumstances become & 
nayal station had been anticipated. 
What the Union Government Is In- 
terested in is the further develop- 
ment of Simonstown. the naval 
base 23 miles from Cape Town, 
which is the headquarters of the 
Africa Squadron, 7 

Mr. Pirow.has intimated that his 
Government would .welcome the 
reinforcement of this squadron, and 


shas even mentioned battleships. 
| simonstown, in short, Is the ob- 


vious point of concentration for 2 
powerful Britlsn fleet in the event 
of our forces being withdrawn from 
the eastern sector of the Mediter- 
ranean and the Suez Canal being 
placed temporarily out of péunds 
for British shipping.” 

NAVAL OPINION 


My suggestion in “The Daily 
Telegraph” of June 8 that such a 
withdrawal. is favoured as an 
emergency measure by a latge body 
of naval opinion, ard that the 
diversion of our eastern traffic via 
the, Cape would not gravely affect 
our imports of food or raw materi- 
als, continues to evoke keen discus- 
sion in Service and shipping circles, 
as shown by the large volume ofi 
correspondence which has been 
received. . ; 

Many correspondents appear to 
regard the evacuation of. the East- 
ern Mediterranean in certain con- 
tingencles as a new and startling 
idea, to which the growth of alr 
power alone has glven rise. 


On the contrary, such ‘a policy 


has ‘been advocated by many 
strategists for nearly 60. years, on 
the grounds that withdrawal would 
confer ‘on theNavy a decisive’ 
strategic | advantage in other’ 
spheres. ; : ' 

AIR DEFENCE IMPRACTICABLE 


In a letter to “The Dally Tele- 
graph” Air Commodore Chamier 
wrongly imputed to me. the sug-, 
gestion that “our ships cannot sall 
seas where their ypresence is re- 
quired.”.. What I indicated was the 
heed for bases which would not be 
exposed to continuous air attack 
from adjacent. territory. That 
form of attack would inevitably dis- 
organise the administration of a 
dockyard port, if only because of 
the effect on the civilian personnel. 

‘The same correspondent suggests 
that -uf.the Navy—ow!ng solely to 
lack of suitable bases—is unable to 
protect the central and eastern 
Mediterranean, the task should be 
entrusted to.air power. 

Unfortunately, aircraft are de- 
pendent on base facilities to an 
even: greater extent than ships. 
How, then, premising Malta to be 
untenable, is the Air Force to pro- 
tect the vital’ passage betweet 
Sieily and Africa if the nearest 
British aerodromes are at’ Alexan- 
dria and Gibraltar. each at least 
800 miles distant from the passage 
to..be protected? 

Air Commodore Chamier argues 
that, “if the Navy 1s to be reduced 
to escort work on the high seas or 
blockade from a great distance, we 
cannot afford to keep it in great 
strength.” But.was it not by exer- 
cising those very functions that the 
British Navy contributed in large 
measure to winning the war? 

WARSHIPS FOR CONVOYS 


’ ‘Mr. EH; Watts, who Is both a 
retired -Naval officer and a pro- 
minent’ shipowner, thinks that 11 
have under-estimated the disloca- 
tion of tradé which would result 
from. re-routing via the Cape. I 
can.only add that my: forecast 1s 
coufirmed by competent authorittes 
who have made 4 special. study of 
the whole question.» arte 
- He asks whether, in the. event of 
a withdrawal ‘from ‘the, Mediter- 
ranean, we should. have sufficient 
warships to convoy the food car- 
riers ‘over, the longer trade route 
via. the Cape. The 
withdrawal would se 
number of warships for employ- 
ment elsewhere. yt 
Further, wher 


‘hold’ the’ Medi! 


volve the use 


every other 


escort work, on 


“ 


have to reckon with heavy casual- 
ties to merchant shipping. During 
the Great War our shipping losses 
in those waters were exceptionally 
severe, and itomay be sald that the 
submarine menace was never really 
overcome as regards the Mediter- 
ranean, " 


NAVAL STRENGTH CHANGE 

‘The purpose of my article was to 
demonstrute that jn certain even- 
tualities it might pay us to with- 
draw our forces. from all but the 
western sector of the Mediter- 
ranean and divert all traffic via the 
Cape, and to show .that such a. 
policy need not have disastrous re- 
sults on our food supply. , 

Our ultimate triumph in a 
struggle of ‘which our. withdrawal 
from the eastern Mediterranean 


| was but an episode would probably 


more than make good any transient 
loss of prestige umong the Eastern 
countries, =e ; 

Several other correspondents 
consider us’ bound alike by his- 
torical. and strategic principles to 
retain our hold on the Mcdlter- 
ranean in all circumstances. They’ 
appear tu be thinking In terms of 
pre-war conditions, when we 
possessed ao substantial margin of 
Naval stréngth: over any” possible 
combination of enemies. 

‘To-day, when we are left only 
with a one-Power Navy; it seems 
unwise to-base our strategy on & 
two-Power standard of strength.

‘GERMANY AND PEACE. 


Delay. In Reply To. Britain: 


) 


"Berlin, June 17... 


Germany’ 3 attention’ is being directed on to-mor- 
ae s debate in London on foreign policy, not in breath. 
less anxiety, but i in a mood of slightly cynical detach-. 


ment, 


~ It is accepted that the first steps; 
will be taken towards the sane 
tion of the venture in sanctions, | 
and from an objective point of | 
view this decision is pronouncéd te! 
be the only right one. The first |” 
steps, {t ts pointed out by ‘German | 
will not, however, | 
carry Great Britaln very far, either 
towards a durable " reconctliatlon 
with Etaly or towards the reform 
of the League and. the establish- 
ment of that new order In Europe 
which It 1s.sought:to associate with 
The leading article of “The! 
in which a_ definite time- 


sequently been appraised as ex- 


‘} pressing sound common sense. 


Unttl the initial phases have been 
passe’ through and the general 
alm: as been more clearly defined, 
the Germans are disposed to sit 
hack and look on. It is dally made 
plainer that Germany cannot give. 
and ought mot to be expectea to 
give, an adequate reply to the Bri- 
tish questions ‘about her “peace 
plan”.until Great Britain's own 
toréian policy and so’ many- other 
obscurities are,‘cleared up, and Bri- 
tish impatience for a reply of any 
sort, which for a short spell was 
seriously considered here. seems to 
be regarded more and more as un- 
necessary or even unregsonable. 

ELASTICITY IN. THE EAST 


“The main lines .Or the reply, as 


der present ‘conditions in view of 
the guiding prindples of National- 
Socialist foreign policy, should by 
now be clear, and nave, indeed, 
been outlined in this correspond- 
ence. No pretence is made that it 
could possibly: fulfil on important | 
points the hopes with which the 
British questions ‘are ussumed to 
have been put, so no ‘point ts seen 
in hastening 1. 

When other’ obstacles have been 
cleared away, it ls intimated, Ger~ 
many will be glatf of a thorough 


reform of the League on the re- 
gional basis which she. herself reg- 
ards as useful only in the ,West; 
but she would’ then expect equal 
consideration to be given to her 
reasons for preferring | individual 
pacts’ of non-aggression in the 
East: Naturally other suggestions 
made by Lord Lothian of a frank 


-eranted that it is 


discussion on the future of Austria, 
Danzig, and Memel, and the possi- 
bilities of economic concessions for 
Germany, including | the colonial 
sphere, are more warmly welcomed. 
But there ts no reason to suppose 
that concessions obtained . along 
such lines, unless they cover u 
much wider ficld than the given 
terms of reference. would suffice to 
bring about a change, in ‘German 
views on the elasticity of frontiers 


In Eastern Europe. With refer- 
ence to such a discussion, 


which. 
according to German ideas, would 
be general only in its subject mat- 
ter and conducted separately with 
each Power, Herr Scheffer says in 
the-“Berliner Tageblatt,” “For Ger- 
many, who knows exactly what she 
wants and’ why. it could oe. be 
welcome.” 

TEACHING OF “MEIN KAMPF”. 

It ts generally admitted that un- 
der present internal conditions 
here no department or group or 
individual, apart from the Fuhrer, 
knows precisely what tactles are 
ip favour, in- foreign policy "from 
day to day, so that if Germany 
“knows exactly what she wants 
and why” in a general strategical 
sense, it can only be through the 
plain principles enunciated by Herr 
Hitler in “Mein Kamp!" tor him-

"TAKEN. TO HOSPITAL 


A ‘distressing . accident occurred 
last night about 9 pm. in the 
vicinity of Cleverly Street, Wanchal. 

A girl, aged about 11 years, was 
walking across the road when she 


.Was knocked down by & tram ‘car. 


She was quickly conveyed to’ the 
Government Civil Hospital 
Up to a late hour last night the 


I etn had not recovered conScious- 


ness,and her name is not known. 

An eye-witness to the accident 
said that the girl apparently did 
not hear the tram car and walked 
into the ‘vehicle while looking in 
the opposite direction. 


OBITUARY 


MR, A. D, CAREY 

By the ‘death’ of Mr. Arthur 
Douglas Carey, which occurred at 
Montreux on June 11 in his 
ninety-second year, England ‘oses 
her oldest. explorer, 

One of a'.famous Anglo-Indlan 
family, the descendants, of Dr. 
William: Carey, of Serampore, he 


was educated at the City of Lon: | 


don School,and, having passed the 
1864 examination for the Indian 
Civil Service, went out at the end 
of 1865°for service in the, Bombay 
Presidency. In 1881 he was made 
Collector of the Salt Revenue and 
‘Acting Commissioner of Inland 
‘Customs in Bombay. © 

Like his missionary. relations in 
later, times, he was Interested in 
North-Western India, and in 1885. 
accompanied ‘by the ill-fated .Mr. 
Dagleish. travelleq from Leh’ in 
Kashmir west of the Kun Lun 
range to Kerla on the North Tibe- 
tan boundary, Thence they turned 
north and reached Khotan in Chi- 
nese Turkestan,, now Sinkiang, 
where they had. a friendly ‘recep- 
tion. From there they worked 
thelr way down the Khotan River 
and to the Tarim R.ver, which,! 
skirting. the Takla Makan Desert, 
empties itself tnto Lake Lob Nor. 
Reaching, Lob Nor towards the end 
of the year, they went: north-west 
to Kuchar and thence by a long 
‘trek through Turkestan. Keeping 
north u. thelr outward route, they 
came home in 1888 by Yarkand 
and Kashmir, having covered some 
3,000 miles In a very little known 
and barren’ part, of Asia, The 


‘| journey was chiefly important for 


the information which both 
Carey and ‘Daglelsh collected about 
the Tarim Basin, a typical Asiatic 
desert, and about the curious sys- 


tem of inland drainage off Lob Nor. 


For this Carey was awarded the 
MacGregor. silver. medal by the 
Royal Georgraphical Society. 

Five years later he was a mem~ 
ber of a new Indian’ Commission 
on,,Customs, Salt, and Opium, and 
fin the same year, 1891, his know- 
ledge of the Bombay district caus- 
ed him to be “sent to Lisbon on 
special duty while the Goa Treaty 
was being negotiated. After the 
conclusion of this treaty in 1893 he 
retired from the Indian Clvil Ser- 
vice, and lived a very secluded life 
with his’sister In Sw'tzerland.

SCHOOL SPEECH 
_. DAYS 


Bishop On False 


Patriotism 


AN APPEAL.TO YOUTH 


(Special Air Mail Service) 


London, June 23,, 

The Bishop of London, Dr. Win- 
nington Ingram, yesterday de- 
clared that he was “galled” by the 
resolution: of the Oxford Union 
“that this house will in no cir- 
cumstances fight for its King and 
country"—when he spoke at the 
Ardingley (Sussex) College prize- 
giving. 

“We want our boys to be brave 
tnough and skilful enough to de- 
fend their country if they are call- 
ed upon to do #0,” he sald. “I 
hate war, but you cannot convince 
Me, we are going to avold war by 
being ashamed ‘to defend our 
country and by letting those armed 
dictators bully. us. 

“Unless. we are.very careful, this 
wave of false patriotism is going 


to do o great, deal of harm. in this. 


country. I ami. glad to see that 
you here have an O.T.C., and while 
we all ought to pray for and work 


for peace, do encourage your sons” 


to be men enough to fight for their 
country when tt comes to the 
point. 

“Had we not taken that line in 
1914 we might have had the Ger- 
man Emperor or Hitler in White 
hall, and I would rather have dear 
old Baldwin.” oe

RAF. ADMINISTRATION 7 


Organisation To Be Split Into 


Four ‘New 


Commands 


(Special Air Mall Service)’. 


\ 


“London, June 23. 


‘Important decisions regarding the reorganisation of 
‘caimanda and administration of home commands, of the 
‘Royal Air ‘Force were announced by the Air Ministry 


yesterday. 

- It is ‘stated that, as a result of 
the expansion of ‘the Force, the. 
Air Council has decided that the 
time has come to carry out cer- 
tain important changes with the 
object of. providing.‘an sppropriate 
organisation for the enlarged Force 
which. will function with speed and 
efficiency in peace and in war. 

One primary feature of the new 


organisation is that it makes the | 


peace organisation conform as 
closely as possible.with the or- 
ganisation visualised for war. As 
training and operations must be - 
separated in war, it is essential 
that the peace organisation should 
give effect to this separation ag’ 
far aa possible. ° " 


THREE OPERATIONAL 
; COMMANDS _ 
The new, organisation provides 
three operational commands and’ 
one training command, as follows: 
Bomber Command, which 


control the ‘bomber squadrons or- | 


ganiseg into a number of groups. 


Fighter. Command, which wilt | Gtoup Headquarters, 


control the, fighter squadrons, the 
army co-operation “squadrons, and 
the Observer Corps. There will be 
two groups of regular fighter | 
squadrons, an army ‘co-operation 
group, and a group for auxiliary 
fighter, and auxillary army ¢o- 
operation ‘squadrons. 

Coastal Command, ‘which will - 
control the’ flying-boat and general 
reconnaissance squadrons’ formed 
into two groups, Certain train- 
ing units have been retained un- 
der this Commad and will be‘ res- 
ponsible for the aministration 
of the Fleet “Alr Arm.. 

‘Training Command, which will 
with a few exceptions, control all 
the training units at home. The 
groups under this*Command will 
consist of one for Flying ‘Training 
Establishments, one for: Groutd 
Training Establishnients, and. one 
Armament Group. The , Super;, 
intendent of ‘the Royal Air Force 
Reserve and Inspector of Civil 
Schools will also come under this 
Command. 

AIR DEFENCE PLANS 


Close Co-Ordination " 


The air defence of. the country 
has been vested in the.Air Defence 
of Great Britain Command and in. 
the’ Coastal Command as hitherto 
constituted. The former of these 
Commands will disappear in its 
present form under the new or- 
ganisation, and the Coastal Com- 
mand will be reorganised: The air 
defence of the country will then 
be vested in the three Operational 
Commands referred to above. . 


Close: “co-ordination. between 


‘these 1s of ‘course, essential, and

upset calculations, * 

National accounts for the first 
quarter of the financial year reveal 
a temporary deficit of” £72,165,000 
which is £15,950,000 above that of 
last year. - 

This is due mainly "to a rise of 
£8,135,000 in expenditure, owing 
to increased defence requirements, 
and a decline in” Tevenue ot 
£7,815,000. 

Commentators are inclined to 
regard: this as ndt too encouraging, 
but poimt out that income tax, 
which for this quarter is not pro- 
perly a rellabla guide, accounts for: 
£4295,000 of the diminution. 


There was also a drop cf £2,650,-. 


000 in estate duties and the effect 
ot the last_budget changes have 
not yet become apparent, 

A better feature is the’ customs 


excise yleld of £74,920,000 com- |‘ 


pared .with £73,726.000, which 1s 
important as an indicator of na- 
tional purchasing power:

provement at £827.255 000 a dimi- 
nution of £75,800.000.—

BRAZILIAN CREDIT 


. » London, June 16. 

Sir Freder.ck Leith-Ross, Ohief 
Economie Adviser to the Govern- 
ment, who last. year went on a 
special mission to China, is return- 
ing to England. Far, from being 
unfrultful his prolonged visit has’ 
promoted in several Important re- 
spects the economic and financial 
affairs of that country. At a cri- 
tical juncture his advice helped 
the Chinese authorities to bring 
about a great and welcome. change 
{n the monetary system, the ad- 
vantages of" wh.ch ar: already be» 
ing felt. A central banking. sys- 
tem is'in course of being estublish- 
ed and a beginning fas been 
made. towards endng the default 
oncertain loans. Another conse- 
quence of Sir Frederlck’s mission Is 
| that the London capital market, 
which ordinarily ts closed ta, for- 
elgn borrowers under the Treasury 
embargo. is belmge opened to facl- 
Utate the Issue.of a railway loan 
lor China. Reference has already 
been made to a sterling loan for 
the completion of the Shanghai- 
Hangchow-Ningpo Railway, which 
Was announced by the Chinese 
Minister of Railways on June 7. 
Now that the issue ls actually be- 
ing ,made in Shanghai—the lists 


4 opened ‘on Saturday and will be 


-closed"there on or befroe Wednes- 
day—it is possible to give further 
particulars of the flotation. The 
nominal amount of th> loan : 
£1,100,000; interest is 6 per cent. 
per annum, and the Issue price: in 
Shanghal ts #4. As previously in- 
timated by’ the press, application 
fs to be made in due course to the 
London Stock Exchange for a 
| quotation of the new loan, Prin- 
cipal and ‘interest of the loan are 
a direct obligation of the Chintse 
National Government and the loan 
is secured in the first place on the 
revenues of the Shanghai-Hang- 
chow-Ningpo Ra‘lway.and on’ rates 
and tolls to be levied upon road 
and rail .traffle over the Chien 
Tang River Bridge, the most. im- 
portant. link in the completion of 
the railway,-.on” the construction 
of ‘which a portion of the, loan 
-funds ig to be expended: The 
average annual ‘revenue for the 


last five years was over $1,500,000. |. 


The amount required annually for 
interest on the loan, during th> 
first five years is, at'an exchange 
rate of"1s. 2d., $1,101,913, so that 
{t will be seen that the specific re- 
venues upon which the loan is 
charged already cover the service 
with a margin, ; : 


SAFEGUARDING PROVISIONS 


The loan agreement dated Ma: 
8. 1936, between the Ministry .o 
Rallways and a syndicate compris- 
ing the British and Chinese Cor- 
poration, Limited, London, and the 
Chinese Development Corporation 
ot Shanghat, which -has been 
formed by the leading Ghinese 
banks, has been duly authorized by 
the Chinese Nat‘onal Government 
,and a copy Nag - been. officially 
communicated to the British Em- 
bassy {n China. The term of ‘the 
loan {s 25 years and repayment of 
the pr-ncipal Js to begin after five 
years’ by annual drawings. The 
total sum required for the annual 
loan service is to be paid over by 
the Shanghai ~ Hangchow - Ningpo 
Railway in monthly instalments in 
sterling to the loan servic2 ac- 


Shanghai Banking Corporation 
and the Bank of China. Al! loan 
service committee 1s to be s2t up. 
as provided for In the 'oan agree- 
Ment, which will -have powers of 


‘| supervision over the railway bud- 


get and management,, This com- 
mittee is to consist of the Minister 


‘| of Rallways“and two representa- 


tives appointed by him and, two 
by the ‘syndicate, and will meet 
monthly. It is also provided in 
the loan agreement that a reserve 
fund Is to be opened for the ac- 
¢umulation of 
yenuey up to the amount required 


for meeting-two annual payments 


of imortization and four half- 
yearly iristalments of interest.”

700, 

The hold of cercain arugs, how-

tunate people, especially in the

Exqguding' alcohol, 
commonest, drugs taken are |,

tuve’ known as dlamorplune hydro- 
chloride, . SW dag

This is unfortunate because tt,

BENEFICIENT DRUGS .

hands, all” these drugs are

known to medicine. ,’ But when

except in increasingly large doses. 

The subsequent reaction in‘las- 
situde and ‘mental depression’ be- 
Mora. 
values become slowly blurred and 
lost. :

ufe tends to become focused on 
cbtaining the'desired drig.at any 
cost--a desire preyed upon: by the 
manufaqhurers and ,.. distributors 
engaged in the trafic. . 
_ Once addicdon has’ been estad- 
lished, the cure of the patient— 
which depends essentially upon 
gradual deprivation of the drug 
concerned—becomes extremely dif- 
fieulc. : 

It may, however, be he:ped. by 
the discovery of the particular 
psychological reason that original- 
ly led to its use, » Thas {s. the 
sense of Infetlority or inade- 
quateness -to some special demand 
in"the sufferer’s life. 

Meanwhile, it canhot be stress-

drugs should be taken excepe 
under medical advice and guper- 
vision, Every measure, individual 
and civic; should, be undertaken 
to eradicate a trade that is.one of 
“the most despicable blots upon 
modern clvilisation. ; :

from British steamers.
